## Game Animation Breakdown

A while back, I asked my patrons for some game animations to analyze, and they came back with some real good ones. So let's look at a few more. Who's first? Excellent choice. Aloy has a lot of traversal animation in her moveset. Climbing, swimming, tightrope walking, etc. But this particular method of traversal is available only in a handful of specific locations. Scattered here and there in this open world's high places are these special rappel points, which you can use to quickly get Aloy down from someplace high. Walk up to one, press the triangle button, and this very flashy rappel animation will play. Once that button is pressed, this whole animation will play out start to finish, and the player won't regain control of Aloy until she reaches the end of the drop. So the functional requirements of this animation are pretty simple. All it really has to do is show her getting from point A to point B and look good doing it. Which it does. The custom camera work on the jump and the grappling hook throw look great. The physicality of that lurch as the hook finds purchase overhead feels right. And I really like the posing work here. I don't know if this particular animation involved any amount of motion capture or if this was all hand keyed, but in either case, the animator has clearly paid a lot of attention to Aloy's posing relative to camera. Just look at her key poses as we go through this whole chain of actions. The jump, the wind up flinging that rope way out to the side, the way she twists her body around for the throw, the way her legs swing out behind her as the rope goes taut, the way she drops one hand and twirls back around for the landing. Just strong posing throughout. This animation also serves as effective characterization. Aloy is extremely practiced and competent. She has been training her whole life to be great at things like this. Even if the player is still getting the hang of the game, you want Aloy's animation to convey her high level of skill. Now, is this jump first, secure your line later rappelling technique practical? Obviously not, and it's not trying to be. There's a fuzzy line between exaggerated competence and impossibly superhuman skill, and the animator's goal when depicting this kind of fantastical skill level is to make it feel believable enough. To make the physicality of it feel more or less correct. To show the character's effort and exertion. To make it feel plausible in a tall tales kind of way. To not worry too much about the fatal levels of rope burn her poor hands have experienced. Or maybe the rope is more of a bungee cord. I don't know, who cares. Now, you can reasonably argue that this animation is stretching most definitions of plausibility pretty far, but it does look cool, and really, it's not that much more absurd than half the other stuff Aloy is capable of in this game. And most importantly, it gets her to her destination quickly. This kind of player experience consideration is one of the many reasons why realism isn't often that useful a lens to look through for gameplay animation. The majority of players do not want to watch a lengthy animation of Aloy carefully securing her line and rappelling down the cliff with proper methodical technique. They want her to get to the bottom of this drop already so they can get back to playing. Speaking of functional concerns, there might be another reason for Aloy jumping so far out into the air before attaching the rope. Now, I didn't work on this, so I can't say this for sure, but I have a hunch that this animation is allowing them not to have to show where the hook attaches. And even if that's not the main reason for her jumping so far away from the rappel point, it is at least a huge benefit. Because it is a lot easier to just show Aloy throwing a line at some unseen target than it would be to show that line connecting to a target and then accurately depicting the rope physics at play as the hook finds purchase and she dangles from the line. This way, there's just fewer elements that have to feel right on camera at once. And that's even more true when you factor in that not all rappel points in this game are stationary. You can also rappel off the top of these tall neck robots, which are very mobile. And factoring in the movement of the thing your rope is attached to and how that would impact the physics of Aloy's descent would be kind of a nightmare. So just having Aloy jump way out here and then having the camera simply focus on her the whole way down cleverly sidesteps those complications. Really, the only issues I can find with this animation are some very minor and kind of understandable polish hitches. I can see several factors here that could make this particular animation very challenging to technically implement in game. It involves the character and game camera covering a large distance in a short time. It has to account for variable distances because some of these rappel drops are higher than others. It involves a character holding and interacting with a rope in literally any way. There is no circumstance in which a character using a rope in a video game is not a giant pain in the butt. But even ignoring the rope, there are some sudden lurching changes in Aloy's position and velocity over the course of this drop that aren't super noticeable, but you do feel them. This first little pop I'm totally fine with. It's an intentionally quick transition from Aloy's locomotion to the start of the rappel. She could be in any number of poses at the exact moment you hit the rappel button, so to some extent, this is unavoidable. But then, as she starts to descend, there's this little pop. This couple of frames where she either moves up or just locks in place while the camera keeps moving down. It's kind of hard to tell. Then there's another big lurch in her position here as she prepares to twirl around. And a final one here just before she touches down. If I had to guess, it looks like these lurches are happening at the seams between each phase of the rappelling motion. Like her falling momentum and her spatial relationship to the camera don't perfectly line up across those seams. Of course, in a film, these sorts of hitches would be much easier to fix, but getting these things to look perfect in a game engine can be quite challenging and consume an enormous amount of time that might be better spent on other things. I think it's telling that this animation has the same pops in the exact same places in the remaster. And in the sequel. And if I'm having to get this nitpicky to find problems, I'd say that this animation is in a pretty great place. Next? I feel like you're being cheeky with this one, but you know what? Run cycles tend to be one of the more important animations on any given game character because you're probably going to be seeing that animation a lot. It is often the foundation of your player character's moveset for getting around the world, which means it's going to have a significant impact on how your character feels to control. ## Which Game Has the Best Dodge Animation?

Which video game has the best dodge animation? I don't know yet, but let's see if we can figure that out. Hi, I'm Dan. I've animated in video games for about 10 years now, and this is my channel about video game animation. Welcome to New Frame Plus. But what even makes a dodge animation in a video game good or bad? Different games can have completely different animation needs. An attack animation which works great in a turn-based RPG will probably work horribly if you just drop that same asset into a fast-paced character action game. An animation which looks fantastic to a first-person camera will very likely look bizarre from any other camera angle. The needs of the game dictate the goals for animation, and an animation's quality ultimately comes down to how effectively it achieves those goals. I usually find it helpful to assess gameplay animation's quality from two angles, function and appeal. Appeal is pretty straightforward. How good does the animation look? Is the motion itself aesthetically pleasing? How well is that animation implemented in-game? Does it transition to and from other animations cleanly during play? Does the animation suit the game's aesthetic? And does the animation suit the character? How much personality does the movement convey? These aesthetic questions are all important, but because we are talking about animations built for gameplay here, there is an even more crucial factor to consider. Functional quality. How well does this animation support the functional needs of the game's play experience? How does the animation impact game feel? Does the animation provide the visual feedback and clarity you need to understand what's happening on screen and react appropriately? When it comes to gameplay, function is ultimately more important than appeal. Because if an animation is not achieving its functional intent in the play experience, whatever that intent might be, then it doesn't really matter how pretty the animation might look because it's making the game experience worse. Now, I have carefully examined the dodge animations from like a hundred different video games for this, many of them recommended by my patrons. My only qualifying criteria were 1. The dodge animation must belong to a player character. So, no animations that are exclusive to NPCs or enemies. 2. It must be a gameplay animation. Nor is this ranking meant to reflect each game's overall quality, because there are plenty of great games with mediocre dodge animations, and vice versa. Cool? Alright. But, remember, in game animation, function is more important than appeal. This is one of those fundamental differences between animation for video games and animation for other media. In gameplay, dodging is mechanically often meant to be a quick reaction the player can make to avoid incoming danger. And if you're hitting the dodge button in a game, you want your character to move now. ## The Animation of Final Fantasy VI
URL: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=N6rtehAI1HE

Final Fantasy VI is a spectacle, a showcase of everything Sakaguchi's team had learned over the last eight years developing these games. Final Fantasy VI further refines these games' evolving approach to character animation, wholeheartedly embracing the character-driven philosophy introduced in Final Fantasy IV. It is the franchise's last mainline entry of the 16-bit era, the last hurrah for 2D pixel art in the core series, and it is, ultimately, a blueprint, the foundation upon which a new era of Final Fantasy games would be built. Welcome to the animation of Final Fantasy. Final Fantasy VI was released in Japan in April of 1994 and in North America six months later. Series creator Hironobu Sakaguchi, now promoted to the role of executive vice president at Squaresoft, was juggling too many responsibilities to directly oversee development at this point. So he handed over the directorial reins to two of his team's rising stars. Hiroyuki Ito, designer of the active time battle format and Final Fantasy V's job system, would take the lead on this new game's battle design. And Yoshinori Kitase, who had contributed narrative and design work to Romancing Saga, Final Fantasy Adventure, and Final Fantasy V, would take point on the story, the game world, and its characters. Final Fantasy VI tells the story of an industrializing fantasy world crushed under the heel of a rapidly expanding empire. A rebel faction calling themselves the Returners struggles in vain to resist this empire's advance, powerless to withstand the invader's technological might. Until, one day, a glimmer of hope appears. A former imperial soldier falls into the Returners' hands, a mysterious woman who can wield magic long thought extinct and who has no memory of who she is. Final Fantasy VI features the series' largest playable cast yet and dedicates a lot of time to their personal stories. According to developer interviews, the creation of this cast was an unusually collaborative effort compared to previous titles, with various team members contributing character and story ideas. The goal, according to Kitase, was to create a protagonist ensemble, a group of equally significant lead characters with none standing out over the rest. I expect he would agree that they didn't quite achieve that goal. Like, I think you'd be pretty hard-pressed to make a convincing argument that the real main character of Final Fantasy VI is Realm, actually. But regardless, I applaud the effort, because even just attempting to make each of these characters individually worthy of the spotlight resulted in the most varied and well-developed cast any of these games had featured yet. An amnesiac woman with powers she barely understands, wielded by others as a weapon all of her life and now paralyzed by apprehension and self-doubt. A treasure hunter slash rebel agent whose jokey demeanor and eagerness to protect belie a life of deep regret. An old-fashioned knight from an already conquered kingdom, haunted by the memories of the home and the family he failed to defend. An imperial general-turned-defector who has to rediscover her sense of self as an individual and fight against the armies she once led. A free spirit, gambler, and theatrical airship captain who abducts women he likes. Okay, that part's not great. The twin princes of Figaro who struggle to accept the royal responsibilities their father's passing has thrust upon them. A feral child abandoned by his father to survive in the wilderness alone. An emotionally withdrawn mercenary with shifting allegiances and no companion but his dog. An elderly goofball descended from ancient mages and forced to live in hiding to conceal those powers from a hostile world. A rambunctious young artist, also descended from mages, who wields her inherited magic through her brush. These three, who are undeniably also in the video game, and of course, the unhinged imperial mage with more personality than every previous Final Fantasy antagonist combined. It is so many characters, and with such a wide variety of backgrounds and behaviors and inner conflicts to untangle, for an animator, this is a lot of great material to work with. Now, up until this point, when discussing the animation in these games, I've put a lot of emphasis on numbers. On counting sprites and poses and the total number of unique frames of animation for each playable character in comparison to those of previous entries. I've done this because, especially this early on, those numbers have been helpful. They have helped to convey not only the animation constraints of the time, but also the slowly increasing animation scope from sequel to sequel. And when you have so few poses to work with per character, seeing which specific actions and activities those precious few sprites are dedicated to portraying can provide some useful insight into what the developers on those early games prioritized. However, we are fast approaching a point in this franchise's history where animation count ceases to be useful information. We are eventually going to be talking about games where the main characters have literally hundreds, if not thousands, of animations each. There comes a point of scale where the quantity matters far less than the quality, how effectively that abundance of animation serves the game's narrative and play experience. So, rather than rattling off increasingly large numbers, let's shift focus to implementation and impact. How does this game's animation build upon previous entries to better achieve its play experience and narrative aspirations? There are a lot of improvements to cover here, but the most impactful difference is a simple change in the approach to character sizing. In every one of these games so far, characters have been displayed at two different sizes for different modes of play. When you were navigating the world, exploring towns, or watching story cutscenes, your characters were displayed as these 16x16 pixel sprites. Then, when it was time for battle, you would get these taller 16x24 sprites, which allowed for more detail and slightly more nuanced posing. Final Fantasy VI breaks tradition here by doing away with these smaller 16x16 sprites entirely. Now, your playable characters are displayed as 16x24 sprites everywhere, whether they're in battle or wandering the world. And this choice to make character scale consistent across all modes of play results in an enormous animation upgrade to pretty much anything that happens outside of combat. Characters' basic locomotion around the world can finally be rendered with the same level of detail those battle sprites have been getting all these years, so now your character can not only have more distinct left and right steps to the stride of their walk, but even little secondary touches like hair swishing back and forth or the flapping of a loose cloak. But even more importantly, this also applies to emote poses. The world and town view is where Final Fantasy characters have been doing all of their acting and expression these last two games. Emote animations have quickly become one of these games' most vital narrative tools, and now those poses can be given more detail and nuance. And on top of that, this overworld sprite size upgrade is also a big win for the non-playable characters. Now even the world's minor NPCs can be given more detail and variety, and they can perform their ambient activities more expressively, making the towns they inhabit more lively and distinct from each other. But it turns out, this change in approach to sprite sizing is actually an enormous upgrade for the battle screen as well. But Dan, I hear some of you saying, the characters in these games have always had 16x24 sprites in combat. This is not new. And you're right, but now that character sprite size is consistent across all modes of the game, that means all of those sprites can now be used anywhere. No longer are characters limited to using battle sprites only in combat and emote sprites only in the worldview. Now characters can stop mid-fight and visibly react to something. Their emote poses can be worked into their special attack animations. Their combat poses can be deployed in story scenes when needed. Now every character has access to their full range of action and expression at all times. And in retrospect, what a wild limitation for the previous game's characters to have had. Put a pin in this because I want to talk more about the way this game works character performance into its battle encounters later, but first we need to dig a little bit further into the sprites those performances are assembled with, because the pose library Kazuko Shibuya designed for Final Fantasy VI's characters achieves something new and very important. As with the previous two games in this franchise, most of your main characters have access to a similar list of emote poses and animations. ## ARCS - The 12 Principles of Animation in Games
URL: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=lOzgxMgAnxQ

Hello, and welcome to New Frame Plus. Let's talk about another of the 12 Principles of Animation.
In previous videos, we explored the principles of timing, squash and stretch, anticipation—you know what? There's a playlist in the description. You don't need me to rattle these off every time. Today, we are going to talk about arcs. It turns out, very few living organisms are naturally capable of movement with direct mechanical precision. Our bodies just aren't built to move in perfectly straight lines on a single axis. Instead, the various parts of our bodies tend to follow these slightly curved paths while traveling from point A to point B. Once the earliest animators had this realization, they increasingly found that describing and charting motion using these curved paths, or arcs, yielded significantly more natural feeling and appealing looking character animation. That's pretty much the whole principle. Arcs describe the curved trajectories which all the parts of our bodies generally follow while in motion. Take walking, for example. If we track the position of various points on the body over time, we will usually find that those points are all following some sort of arcing path. This hand swinging back and forth? That's an arc. The elbow further up the arm? Also an arc. The heel of the foot? It's got an arcing path of its own. And even the pelvis, our center of gravity, follows a smoothly oscillating path up and down with each stride. For the animator, these curves are a very useful tool for both understanding and recreating human body mechanics in our animation. And it kind of makes sense that our bodies move this way when you think about it. I mean, so much of our skeletal structure is basically these series of joints one after another, each rotating or hinging at the sockets, each creating an arcing motion as they do so. No wonder it's so hard to move any part of our body in a perfectly straight line. Every joint involved in creating that motion is moving in an arc. This is another one of those animation principles which is largely about mimicking the rules of physics and the natural mechanics of our anatomy. Arcs help us to properly convey mass and the forces acting on that mass. If an animated character's movements don't follow arcs, if they just mechanically trace a linear path from one key pose to the next one, the results will feel stiff and incorrect. The weight of it will feel all wrong. But arcs aren't just important because they make organic motion look realistic. They also tend to be aesthetically pleasing to the eye. Smooth arcs lend animated motion a sense of fluidity that we really tend to like. And what's more, arcs can be exaggerated to push that appeal even further. Taking the arcs of motion our bodies naturally follow and amplifying them can make your character animation feel even more dynamic or graceful. It just tends to look really cool. Even when working with naturalistic characters and motion capture, smoothing and rounding out the arcs of their motion just a little bit can often make that animation look better. Weirdly, it can sometimes even make that motion feel more, quote, realistic to our eyes than the raw motion capture data looked. It's an odd phenomenon. Of course, the animator should be careful not to go overboard with these things. Real organic motion may follow arcs, but it is still complex, and filtering all of that complexity down into nothing but smooth, clean arcs can make that motion start to feel unnaturally floaty or weightless. And there are scenarios where you might not want your character's movements to follow an arc. Perfectly linear motion might feel pretty lifeless and mechanical, but if you're animating a machine of some sort, that might be exactly the look you want. Or maybe the object you're animating is being impacted by an external force, which would interrupt the smooth, arcing trajectory of their movement. Or maybe not having an arc would look really funny. Sometimes unnatural, incorrect motion just looks funny. Now, for the most part, arcs in game animation function in pretty much the same way as animation anywhere else. They make character motion look more natural and more appealing. And they can be instrumental in lending video game action more visual clarity. Have you ever wondered why so many attacks in 3D games involve these big swipes rather than more direct stabbing motions? It's because a stab only involves a single forward axis of motion, whereas a swipe, an arc, involves more of them, meaning that attack is going to be easier to read from more camera angles. And some game animations happen so fast that adding a motion trail, making the action's most important arc plainly visible to all players, can make that action read far clearer. And look cooler. Likewise, interrupting and breaking arcs can be a great way to create contrast. When a character gets hit in an action game, most of the tricks we use to emphasize that impact—the quick snap to an exaggerated hit react animation, the hit stop, the shake—all of these tricks violently interrupt whatever arcs of motion preceded that impact, further emphasizing the hit. Of course, game animation does add some fun complications when it comes to applying this principle. For one thing, we don't always get to know where the camera is going to be. When you're animating characters in a film, you can fine-tune all of your arcs to look extra great to the only camera angle the audience is ever going to see. But if you happen to be animating on a 3D game with a player-controlled camera, well, the only way to make sure that your animation's going to look good is to try to make those arcs look appealing from all angles, which can be tricky. And even after you have meticulously polished the arcs on every animation asset in the game, then you still have to contend with the challenge of making the arcs in those individual animations flow organically into one another. In an interactive context where the player is effectively choosing which animations happen and when. This has been one of game animation's most daunting technical challenges for a long time. Because the human eye is annoyingly good at spotting those little erratic pops and jerks that can happen when parts of the body suddenly lurch from one arcing path to another one. And because arcs are so important to helping us sell a character's weight and size, the bigger and heavier the character is supposed to feel, the more those erratic pops between animations are going to just wreck the character's sense of weight. We do have tools for mitigating this problem, such as blends, transitions, inverse kinematics, real-time physics objects attached to the character which can provide some overlap continuity across the seams, all manner of complex animation systems under the hood, but this particular struggle is one that game animators are unfortunately probably never going to be completely rid of. As is often the case with animation for video games, creating an enjoyable interactive experience for the player sometimes requires aesthetic compromises. Even so, arcs are fundamental to the motion of the human body, and most organic creatures' bodies, for that matter. And the more we mind the arcs in our character's animation, the more natural and appealing their motion will look. And with that, we have now covered half of the 12 Principles of Animation. If you have found these videos informative or helpful, then please consider subscribing or supporting the show more directly, like all of these wonderful patrons listed here. Thank you very much for watching, and I will see you next time.

## The Animation of Final Fantasy V
URL: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=D3jmQDEQ-fo

Thus far in this animation retrospective, we've seen significant strides in the evolution of Final Fantasy's narrative toolkit. We've seen the earliest uses of gameplay systems to stage key story moments. We've seen the first story cutscene. And we've seen the emergence of actual characters, and of stories which primarily focus on those characters. There's just one problem. The animation in these games is not really contributing much on that front. As you have no doubt noticed, the evolution of Final Fantasy's animation across these first four entries has been incremental at best. We spent three entire games with a playable cast who were capable of only two things, walking and fighting. We've only just started to see characters with the ability to visibly emote at all. The basic foundation of the Final Fantasy narrative experience may be taking shape, but this new character-centric approach can only go so far without characters who can actually express a full range of emotion. It's time for the animation to start pulling some more weight. Welcome to the animation of Final Fantasy. Final Fantasy V was released in Japan in 1992, and wouldn't see a Western localization until the Final Fantasy Anthology port for the PlayStation in 1999. As Final Fantasy games are wont to do, this game tells a story of magic crystals under threat. The Wind Crystal has suddenly shattered, and all across the world the breeze has stilled. Almost as if in response to this calamity, a strange meteor falls from the sky, an event which will ultimately draw together four heroes who must find a way to protect the remaining three crystals and fight back against the ancient evil they are secretly sealing. It is a somewhat stock premise, as Final Fantasies go, but that's fine, because we are in a post-Final Fantasy IV world, and this ship runs on characters now. You've got Lena, Princess of the Kingdom of Tycoon. You've got Faris, a pirate captain who is also a princess of Tycoon, it's complicated. There's Gallif, an amnesiac man found unconscious at the meteor crash site. And of course there's, um, Bartz, a wanderer who gets swept up in all this by chance. Oh, and also there's Gallif's granddaughter, Kryle. Can't forget her. It's a much smaller playable cast than we saw in Final Fantasy IV, and that's mostly for one very practical reason that we'll talk about in a minute, but you get to spend a lot of time with this bunch, and they all get a lot of time to know each other. This is in part because unlike Final Fantasy IV, which mostly centered its story around a single character, Final Fantasy V's ensemble tends to share the spotlight. But it's also because Final Fantasy V is simply a much bigger game. Squaresoft ramped up production big time for Final Fantasy V. The development team tripled in size, going from a headcount of 14 to 45. The game itself shipped on cartridges with twice the memory capacity, allowing that larger team to squeeze even more stuff into the game. And that increased budget is noticeable. Where Final Fantasy IV often looked like a slightly enhanced port of one of the NES titles, this feels like a proper 16-bit upgrade. The background art is lavish, the story cutscenes and the complexity of their presentation has increased significantly, and the sprite count per character? Well, let's just get into it. Final Fantasy V represents the largest increase in animation scope this franchise has seen yet, and the main reason for that is the return of the job system. First introduced in Final Fantasy III, the job system allowed playable characters to swap combat classes whenever the player wanted. Any of your characters could be switched to any job, but you will recall that this feature had a significant narrative drawback. It turned Final Fantasy III's characters into completely blank slates, a quartet of faceless combat units entirely defined by their current job. There was nothing more to them than their name and whatever hat you were making them wear. Final Fantasy IV had approached characters completely differently. On top of having unique visual designs and personalities, each character's combat role was predetermined, unchangeable, and integral to their character. Every character's combat abilities helped to give them identity, to define them. Final Fantasy V takes a hybrid approach. These characters have predetermined names, except for Bartz, I guess, and they all have unique designs. And they all can switch jobs at will, but when they do, though they will change into an appropriate costume, they remain recognizably themselves. No matter what job you choose for these characters, they will always be visually distinct from their companions. Bartz will look like Bartz, whether he is a knight or a monk or a chemist or a ranger. If you decide to make both Lena and Faris be red mages, they will continue to look like Lena and Faris, but in cooler hats. You can turn your entire party into the same class, and they will continue to be easily recognizable as Bartz, Lena, Faris, and Gallif. And also Krile. And that persistence of character didn't come cheap. There is no clever trick at play here. This was simply achieved by creating a full set of combat sprites for every playable character for every single job in the game. Take Lena here. This is her default or freelancer class design, and it comes with the standard suite of poses. An idle pose, a walking pose, a ready or defending pose, an attack pose, two more poses for attacking with a left-handed weapon, two spell charging poses, an ability or victory pose, a hit react pose, a wounded pose, and a knocked out pose. But then say you decide to make Lena be a white mage. Well, she's got a whole new set of sprites specifically for that job. Or maybe you make her into a summoner. Or a dragoon. Or a blue mage. No matter which class you choose, she has got a full set of sprites for it. Except for the knockout pose. All of the jobs just use the freelancer version for that one. This functionally means that where Final Fantasy IV's characters each had about 12 total frames of animation for combat, Final Fantasy V's player characters have 11 unique frames per job. And with 22 total jobs included in the game's original release, plus that one shared knockout pose, that means that each playable character has up to 243 unique frames of animation just for combat alone. 243, my mathematically inclined friends have assured me, is a much larger number than 12. Kazuko Shibuya, who hadn't done any sprite work for Final Fantasy IV because she'd been busy working on Romancing Saga at the time, split this massive character art workload with Hiromi Ito. And even combining their efforts, it still took the two of them an entire year to churn through the sprite art for these jobs. But the payoff, I would argue, is well worth that scope explosion because, thanks to this enormous sprite library, Final Fantasy V can offer all of the gameplay flexibility of a job system while simultaneously enjoying all of the narrative benefits of maintaining a cast of consistently defined characters. And that's not the only win here. Look a little closer at all of these sprites and you'll see that there's actually a fair amount of posing variety from character to character and from class to class. Bartz casting a spell as a freelancer is posed differently than Bartz casting a spell as a black mage. Bartz casting a spell as a red mage looks different than Bartz casting a spell as a monk. And what's more, Bartz casting a spell as a monk is also posed differently than Lena casting a spell as a monk. Or Gallif. Or Ferris. Or Krile. Or look at the hit reacts. Look at how much variety there is in the posing, not only across each class a character might be at any given time, but also across every other character's unique incarnation of that class. Pretty impressive, given these sprites are still just 16 pixels wide and 24 tall. The one thing that would have put this over the top for me is if this varied posing was more driven by character personality. Because that pose variety, though great, does often feel more arbitrary than character-driven. Of course, previous Final Fantasies fared little better on this particular front, but the absence of consistent characterization feels a little more noticeable this time because these characters can change combat roles so frequently. Final Fantasy IV got so much mileage out of using each character's job to communicate more about them, but Final Fantasy V's playable characters just can't be defined by their job. They change jobs constantly. It's entirely under the player's control. And given that, it's a bit of a shame that there's so little consistent characterization happening in each character's posing across all of those jobs. Now, Shibuya-san has mentioned in interviews that character personality was very much something she was thinking about as she designed the sprites for each job, and I believe her. Especially in the costume designs, you can clearly see specific choices being made for how each character wears each role. But whether it's due to the constraints of sprite size or simply a consequence of having to generate so many job variants of each pose for each character, it is difficult to find discernible posing trends across these jobs, which might help to convey any given character's unique personality. Like, Faris and Galuf might cast spells differently as Dragoons, but nothing about these poses is expressing who these two are as people. Even so, the enormous effort required to keep Bartz, Lena, Faris, Galuf, and Kryle recognizable across 22 jobs achieves exactly what it needs to, because at the end of the day, the entire point of all of this work is to keep the characters in the spotlight, to not let the fun gameplay feature obscure their presence or compromise their individual value to the story. But while this scope increase is impressive, the game's biggest animation win happens outside the battle screen.
It was Final Fantasy IV which first introduced emote poses to the series, additional unique sprites which finally allowed

## The Animation of Sonic Frontiers
URL: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Tisc5iZKqS0

I am beginning to realize the severity of the mistake I've made. You see, I thought I was making a video assessing the unpredictable quality of animation in Sonic the Hedgehog games, but what I did not realize in that fit of creative enthusiasm and, let's be honest, morbid curiosity, was that I had just stepped into the Sonic YouTube equivalent of quicksand. Make no mistake, I am not a Sonic the Hedgehog fan, although when I say that, people seem to have a difficult time believing me, and yeah, okay. But whether or not I like Sonic is no longer relevant, because now I have become one of those people who talks about Sonic. And Sega is never going to stop making Sonic the Hedgehog games, so I'm stuck here now. In releasing that video, I entered the Hedgehog's gravitational pull, and I do not have an exit strategy. Sonic died this year, and yet here I am. Nothing changed. What I'm trying to say is, welcome to New Frame Plus, let's talk about the animation in Sonic Frontiers. As we covered last time, good Sonic game animation boils down to four things. Appeal, characterization, game feel, and polish. But different games have different animation needs, and Sonic Frontiers is a rather different kind of Sonic game. So let's dig into this, starting with... This is a very important category of animation for any Sonic game, because it's what you're going to spend the majority of your time doing, running and jumping. But Sonic Frontiers has you running and jumping across a large, open 3D environment, with mostly complete freedom to explore in any direction you want. And historically speaking, high-speed freeform exploration is not something that Sonic games do well. 3D Sonic has usually worked best when he is confined to a track, when the only direction available is either forward or generally to the right. Take those rails away, and things tend to get very slippery very quickly. So with the constraint of that track removed, and the player now in full control of both speed and direction, Sonic's handling has to be far more carefully tuned, and his animation has to be a lot more adaptable and do a lot more heavy lifting to provide players the clear visual feedback they need to be able to feel in control during play. To be honest, when I first saw that Frontiers was going to be an open-world platformer, I was pretty confident predicting this was going to be a mess. But, lo and behold, Sonic's running and jumping in Frontiers is pretty darn solid, actually. Now, this is largely a technical animation victory. If you look at each of Sonic's animations individually, the jump, the run, the walk, a lot of them are very similar to what we have seen in previous 3D entries. They all look fine, but I don't think they are the reason this feels better. The difference is in the implementation. Sonic's animation here is very responsive to player input, quickly conveying his direction, current speed, and which traversal state he is in, whether that be running, sprinting, jumping, bouncing, rail grinding, whatever. And, crucially, his animation, when combined with the effects and the camera and the audio, effectively sells the exhilaration of speed without actually zipping you through this world so quickly that you feel completely out of control. Of course, achieving this comes at a bit of aesthetic cost. Sonic's ability to change direction almost instantaneously or quickly snap from the animation of one locomotion state to another one with almost no blend or transition between does mean sacrificing some sense of physicality and realistic weight for the character, but in a game where the player may be counting on that clear visual feedback to make split-second input decisions, that is a very worthwhile tradeoff. Granted, the 3D Sonic jank is still here. I'm not saying it's not. Things tend to get real buggy looking at slow speeds, and Sonic transitions out of some animations way, way too early, snapping from one state to another one well before the previous animation was done. Also, they dropped the squetch on his spinball jump, I assume just to spite me personally. Wait, correction. There is a little bit of squash and stretch on the jump, specifically when you double jump out of a rail grind, or also when you have double jumped and you hold down A again so you can sort of charge up a little spin dash before you land, then there's a little bit of squetch on the Sonic ball too. I'm not sure why it's not on any of the other jumps. I'm guessing it must be a bug. I don't know. Whatever. I want the squetch back on the jump is my point. It was good. Ultimately, we have seen more polish, appeal, and even personality from Sonic's locomotion and traversal in previous games, but even with those rough edges, no other 3D Sonic has made this feel good before. Not even close. And that is such a game feel victory for this franchise that I'd still call this a win. But okay, that's just locomotion. What about... I think it's important to understand, combat in most Sonic games is not complicated. Enemies aren't put in these levels so you can fight them. These aren't duels in the field of honor. These enemies are speed bumps. Half the time, you're only attacking them for platforming purposes. Fighting generally just isn't the point in a Sonic game. And in the past, when a Sonic game has tried to make combat be a more robust aspect of what is still fundamentally a running and jumping game experience, that has not gone well. Except for the boars. The boars were perfect. So here again, Sonic Frontiers is taking on a pretty scary challenge for this franchise by attempting to make prolonged battles one of the load-bearing pillars of the play experience. This world is littered with enemies who can take a lot of hits, who each represent a kind of combat puzzle for the player to solve. And this greater emphasis on combat necessitates a considerably wider range of combat moves for Sonic. So now, Sonic's homing attack, which has basically been 3D Sonic's main way of attacking enemies since Sonic Adventure, has now been turned into just the distance-closing opener for a brand new attack combo. And this combo feels quite good. Each individual attack might be a bit hard to make out in the flurry of motion and hit effects, but thanks in part to the camera swinging out to the side to give us a better angle, the way Sonic quickly snaps to and holds on each heavily exaggerated pose adds just enough clarity for your brain to connect the dots between each move. And as you progress further into the game, you will unlock more and more increasingly flashy combat moves. A lot of effort and attention has clearly gone into making these feel cool. And while I do think having a lot of your moves essentially be micro-cutscenes you have to watch every time you use them can get old after 20 or 30 times, the presentation on these is executed with so much more meticulous care than anything the previous games have delivered. Sonic's new suite of combat animations is, at least in terms of visual appeal and relative polish, one of this game's animation strengths, I'd say. But if we're going to talk combat spectacle, we also need to talk about... Sonic Frontiers aims for spectacle with its boss encounters in a way that no other Sonic game has ever really pulled off. And while that spectacle may not always result in fantastic play I honestly found like half of these really irritating, if we're talking strictly in terms of visual presentation, for Sonic Team, this is a noticeable step up. Sonic Frontiers, broadly speaking, is aiming for a grander sense of scale than you get in most Sonic experiences, and these fights are where those efforts are felt most clearly. And the biggest contributor to that difference, the real animation MVP in Sonic Frontiers, is the camera. The way an action is staged, the choice of camera angle and framing, the cinematography, can single-handedly make or break the impression that action leaves. I mean, just look at the difference between what this Street Fighter attack feels like with its custom camera, and then without it. And across the board, I see so much more intent and care given to the staging of Sonic Frontiers' action, to the dance happening between the boss' motion and the camera framing them, and all the little ways the camera works to make so many of these boss attacks and moments feel so much bigger and cooler. Of course, the execution on the animated spectacle in these fights is not consistent across the board. This gameplay camera, for all of its aesthetic improvements, is not always your ally. And a lot of these battles have trouble finding the appropriate balance between spectacle and clarity. I mean, this is just noise. Also, it's pretty clear that this game just cannot technically deliver on all of the big moments this team's aiming for, no matter how powerful the hardware you're playing on. And honestly, even with all of this cool-looking stuff, there is still so much room for improvement, so much more refinement that could happen with some extra polish time. Even so, this kind of shounen anime action is a real good fit for Sonic. This feels so right. Like, this character's been going Super Saiyan since day one. If any retro platformer mascot should be doing cool anime fights, this is your guy. But that brings me to... As we have already discussed at length, Sonic games do not have a stellar history when it comes to cinematics and narrative animation in general. The high point for Sonic cutscene animation was Sonic Boom, a game that was otherwise kind of a trainwreck. And the low points have been truly dire. Uncanny motion capture making everyone look like humans wearing mascot suits, giant faces that barely animate, giant faces that animate entirely too much. But really, we haven't seen a true cinematics disaster from Sonic since 2006. No, in recent years, the narrative animation

But as long as we are recognizing excellence, let's just go ahead and give Arc System Works their annual trophy for DNF Duel. ## The Animation of Final Fantasy IV

There are a lot of good reasons to like Final Fantasy games. Their settings are imaginative and rendered with such enthusiasm. Their combat and progression systems are robust and varied. No two games play exactly alike. The stories they tell are grandiose in scale, and the music is consistently gorgeous. Doesn't matter which one you're playing, it's like Sonic. Even the games you don't like have some great music in them. Any of these reasons is enough to pick up and enjoy a Final Fantasy. But if there is one element that the games in this franchise truly live or die on, it is the appeal of their characters. Characters are what get you emotionally invested in that grandiose story's dramatic stakes. They are what make you care. And if any given entry can't get you invested in at least one or two of the main cast personalities, all of those other great elements are going to be fighting a real uphill battle to keep you playing for 30-plus hours. Final Fantasy IV is an important moment for this franchise, because it's the first game in the series to truly put its characters at center stage and shine a spotlight on them. Final Fantasy IV is where Final Fantasy becomes itself. Welcome to the animation of Final Fantasy. Final Fantasy IV was released in Japan on the Super Famicom in 1991, and in North America on the Super Nintendo Entertainment System later that same year. This is the franchise's first time making the leap to a new, more powerful generation of home console gaming hardware, and that's always an exciting time. For all of the improvements we've talked about thus far in each successive Final Fantasy game, all of those gains and refinements were achieved within the same technological constraints, which is impressive. But now, thanks to this beautiful new matte gray and purple brick, finally we can see what this team can achieve with some of those old constraints removed, the boundaries of potential expanded. It's the start of an exciting new era for Final Fantasy, so let's talk about what that fancy new hardware can do. Rather than doing a full technical breakdown of the thing, let's just focus on the upgrades that are going to make the biggest difference for animation. The first of these, and I would argue the most important, was color. You may recall that the Super Nintendo's predecessor offered a palette of 54-ish usable colors, of which it could only display at most 25 or so at a time. That was a very tight constraint. The Super Nintendo bumped that color count up to around 32,000, and it could display 256 of those colors at once, ten times what the NES had been capable of. And with the ability to use a whopping 16 colors in a single 8x8 tile, up from the four they used to be limited to, artists could now pack far more detail into each tile. The Super Nintendo didn't actually render a higher resolution image than the NES did. Its 256x224 resolution is more or less identical to what the previous console offered. All of the additional detail and richness you see here comes exclusively from that expanded color palette. The second major improvement the Super Nintendo brought, and this one's much more straightforward, was sprite count. While the Super Nintendo's sprites were still generally constructed of those same 8x8 tiles the NES offered, the Super Nintendo could handle twice as many on screen at one time, up to a maximum of 128. And you could now have four times as many of those sprite tiles occupying the same horizontal scanline at once, up to a new maximum of 32. Now I should note that I am oversimplifying a lot on this one. Technically your sprite objects could be much bigger than 8x8 if you wanted, and you just had to use fewer of them on screen at once, but you get the idea. Way more sprite tiles on screen than before. And the third major improvement the Super Nintendo offered was background modes. You may recall the NES didn't give you a lot of options when it came to rendering backgrounds. You had one layer, it could only use 13 colors, and all you could really do with it was have it scroll in various directions as one big chunk. The Super Nintendo, on the other hand, was capable of rendering up to four background layers, allowing for far more complex multi-layer movement, like the parallax scrolling you see happening with these two slightly offset layers here. And these multiple layers were far more versatile too. The Super Nintendo offered a variety of background display modes, each mode providing a different combination of layer counts, color counts, and scrolling options. This allowed the developers to select the ideal mode for their specific needs at any given moment. Say you wanted to have the most background layers possible. Well, mode 0 would give you all four possible layers to work with, but each layer only had access to less than 32 colors in total, and you could only use four of those colors per tile. If you wanted more colors than that, then you could try mode 1. This was by far the most commonly used option, which only gave you three layers, but two of them would have access to 128 total colors, and you could use up to 16 colors per tile. You get the general idea. There were eight such modes in total, including the one that most folks actually know about, mode 7. Mode 7 was special because while it gave you only a single background layer with 256 colors, that one layer was highly malleable. It could be rotated, skewed, or stretched at will, and when used creatively, this mode could suggest the illusion of perspective, enabling all of those faux 3D backgrounds we remember so fondly. The Super Nintendo Entertainment System came with plenty of other upgrades and improvements, of course, but for animation, those are the big ones, and Final Fantasy IV makes a commendable effort to take advantage of these expanded resources. I mean, just look at this character sprite. This combat pose is 24 pixels tall and 16 pixels wide, and if those numbers sound familiar, it's because this is the exact same size as the fighter from Final Fantasy I. The only difference here is the number of colors the artists were allowed to use, and as you can see, the difference is significant. Thanks to this relatively enormous new color palette, every character and enemy and town and dungeon in this game can be painted in significantly more detail. And not just more detail, but more variety. Just look at the cast of Final Fantasy II. The actual Final Fantasy II. You know what I mean. Compared to this bunch of characters, look how much more visually distinct each member of your party can be when they have so many more colors simultaneously available. Plus, being able to use 10 times as many colors as before also means that now we can have full-color backgrounds in combat, rather than the flat, empty nothing zones of the NES days, allowing the battle screen to better reflect the environment of your party's current location. And being able to use way more tiles on the sprite layer pays off in all kinds of neat ways. You want to pack more characters on screen at once? Not a problem. When you cast a spell, the effects can be bigger and flashier. Look at how much more complex summon spells are starting to look. And with all of these extra sprites to work with, your enemies don't have to live on the background layer anymore. Now they can finally join your party members on the sprite layer, which means you could actually animate them. Granted, Final Fantasy IV doesn't really do anything with that. The enemy sprites continue to be mostly static images during battle, but those sprites are getting very detailed and pretty. And the flying enemies sometimes, like, float up and down a tiny bit. The point is they're not stuck on the background anymore. Which is good, because that means the artists can start having more fun with that background. I mean, they can do this for a battle environment, and this looks cool. Thanks to all of those background modes and additional layers, now dungeons can start having more depth to them, creating a greater sense of scale. And when you take flight in an airship, they can go all Mode 7 on you, tilting the world map to give the impression of elevation and a horizon line just offscreen. Now, these are all welcome upgrades, but one could argue that this isn't really a huge leap beyond the games which came before, right? Yes, the environments are more detailed and the spells are flashy, and the characters are more distinct, and that's all great, but the resulting product does kind of end up looking like a 16-bit paint-over of one of the old 8-bit games a lot of the time, doesn't it? Like a hyper-literal answer to the question, what if an NES game was allowed to have a lot more colors? Not to get ahead of ourselves, but as the next two games will soon demonstrate, much potential remains to be tapped here. And yet, despite that, Final Fantasy IV as an experience feels significantly unlike its predecessors, and that is because, just like with Final Fantasy II, it is all about the presentation. Final Fantasy IV was created by a team of around 14 people over the course of one year, back in a time when you could make a Final Fantasy game with around 14 people over the course of one year. It was directed, once again, by series creator Hironobu Sakaguchi, but much of the moment-to-moment narrative work of script writing and scene blocking and presentation was in the hands of lead designer and scenario writer Takashi Tokita. This was actually Tokita's first project as a full-time employee at Squaresoft. He'd originally moved to Tokyo with the intent of being a stage actor and started working on games as a way to pay the bills. And, in retrospect, I could have just stopped researching the development of this game right after reading that fact, because learning that this game's creative direction was in the hands of Mr. Story and the Theater Kid kind of tells you everything

They all have a walking pose, which can cycle with the default pose to create their walking animation. But that standard set of poses isn't going to be quite enough to cover Final Fantasy IV's needs, and this is for two reasons. The first is Final Fantasy IV's brand new active time battle system, which introduces a loose approximation of real-time action to the franchise's turn-based battles. Where previous games would pause after each round of combat to allow the player to give everybody commands, now time flows more or less continually, with each combatant having to wait a certain amount of time between attacks, and the player entering commands to individual party members as each one becomes ready to receive new orders. The removal of that specially designated command phase introduces an element of chaos and time pressure to battles in Final Fantasy, and with this party of up to five characters accepting commands at variable times, it becomes pretty helpful for the player to have a clear visual indication of which characters have and haven't been given commands yet. And so, in addition to all of the poses I listed before, every playable character has been given between one and three new poses for when that character has received your command and is just waiting for their turn to execute it. Let's call it the ready pose. Now every character has a standard ready pose, which is generally used when they are preparing to do a physical attack or use an item. This is also the pose that they'll strike when you order them to defend for a turn, so they're getting a lot of mileage out of this one. But certain members of your party have an additional variant of this, a special ready pose, if you will. They might even have a pair of them built to cycle and make the character appear to be preparing a spell. You will generally see these when the character is about to cast some sort of magic or use some other unique ability. But that is not the only change to the battle animation in Final Fantasy IV. In fact, there's a rather fun new trend just starting to emerge in the animation in these games, and to talk about it, we need to go back to Final Fantasy III for a second. If you've been watching this series, something that you'll have probably noticed in the animation of those 8-bit games we've already talked about is that in each of those games, there was a fairly rigid framework that every character's animation stuck to. Every playable character would have the exact same list of combat poses as their party members, and those poses would get used in pretty much the exact same way. Step forward, attack, victory, it was all very structured and homogenous. The only animation differences from character to character were in the details of the sprite art itself. This worked perfectly fine for those games, and Final Fantasy III used that same formulaic approach for its enormous pile of job classes. Except, every now and then, it didn't. Very occasionally, you would see a particular job perform a specific action in a unique way. Just as an example, say you had a Magus in your party. Whenever you had them cast a black magic spell, they would generally walk forward and strike this pose with some colorful spell-appropriate effects layered over them. Then they would go back to their idle pose as the spell effects fly, and eventually they'd return to their place in line. But if you had them cast the black magic spell Quake, they would do something slightly different. They would walk forward, strike the pose with the spell cast effect, and then perform their weakened pose, which in that context instead read as them putting their hands to the ground and causing the resulting earthquake effect. We've seen combat poses used for multiple purposes before, but this was different. A pose uniquely repurposed for a specific spell which only a few jobs in the game could access. And the Dragoon took this much further, because if you told them to do their jump attack, they would step forward and hit their mark, just like every character does when it's their turn to go, but then they would strike that weakened pose for just a moment, in this case using it as a crouch, before springing off screen in their left-hand attack pose. Then a short while later, they would crash down on the enemy's head in a pose completely unique to this one attack, bouncing back into the sky and then returning to land in their equivalent of a spell casting slash victory pose. So that is a clever asset reuse, and that's a custom pose dedicated to one specific attack, and that's a unique way of moving around the battle screen, all for one move. Now, for Final Fantasy III, all this ultimately amounted to was a few very slightly fancier combat animations. Pattern deviations so minor and infrequent that you might not have even recognized they happened as you entered commands in your muscle memory trance, just trying to get through this random battle. But in the larger arc of Final Fantasy combat animation, that was an exciting first. A custom animation that only one character has access to. A tiny deviation from that rigid, uniform combat animation structure. And as we can now see in Final Fantasy IV, those deviations are starting to snowball into a trend, because now every one of the characters in your party has one or more special abilities that only they can do, with custom special ability poses and animation to help those unique moves stand out. Rydia has a pose just for casting summon spells, because she's a summoner, like her mom. Edge has a unique pose for using throwing weapons like shuriken, because he's a ninja. Edward has a pose for singing, because he's a bard, and he has another one for hiding, because he's a bard who's also a chicken. And Cain is a dragoon, so of course he's got an improved version of that Final Fantasy III jump, complete with a new, significantly more dynamic custom pose for the landing. ## The Animation of Final Fantasy III
URL: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=BpHnBv4PZGU

There's always something special about a franchise's last game on any given console. The developers have a game or two under their belts, they've had years to get comfortable with all of the quirks and capabilities of the hardware, and now they can just focus on iterating and expanding on the ideas that have already proven successful. The 8-bit era has been an important one for the animation in Final Fantasy. The first game established a foundation, with animation focused strictly on the functional needs of traversal and combat. The second shifted focus to story, completely overhauling the first game's approach to narrative presentation. And now here with the third, it's all about refinement. Oh, and a huge increase in scope. That too. Welcome to the animation of Final Fantasy.
Final Fantasy III launched on the Famicom in 1990, and wouldn't get an official release in North America for a very long time. This was the first entry in the series to feature a job system, allowing you to swap your playable characters to different classes and optimize your party composition for any given challenge. It is so much more fun to play than its predecessor, but the return of clearly defined combat roles in this new system does come with one unfortunate tradeoff.
The downside to Final Fantasy III's otherwise delightful job system is that, unlike the playable characters of Final Fantasy II, your party of Light Warriors are now back to being completely devoid of personality. Don't get me wrong, Final Fantasy II's cast didn't have much characterization going for them either. I spent more than 50 hours with these people, and I couldn't give you more than two sentences about their personalities. But they were still distinct individuals with their own looks and traits and history, and they each had a real stake in the larger story happening around them. Not so with the cast of Final Fantasy III. You still get to name them, and they do at least start the game with some palette-swapped coloring to tell them apart, but even that disappears ten minutes in once you unlock the first set of jobs for them to swap to. All of them will look like this as a warrior. All of them will look like this as a monk. They will completely vanish into whatever combat role you have currently assigned them. And it's not just their designs. These four get zero characterization whatsoever. When your party members speak, the game doesn't even specify which of them is talking. In terms of character, at least, it's a pretty big step back, one which the 3D remake of this game would later go out of its way to rectify. But though the return of combat roles might have come at the cost of personality this time, it sure does crank up the variety.
The leap in animation scope from Final Fantasy II to Final Fantasy III is kind of enormous, and that increase is almost entirely thanks to this job system. Final Fantasy I had six playable characters, and Final Fantasy II boosted that count to nine. But the playable characters of Final Fantasy III are able to swap between 22 different jobs, each of which has its own unique set of sprite art, which basically means that Final Fantasy III has not four, but 22 playable characters to animate. And what's more, those 22 playable characters have even more frames of animation. Where Final Fantasy I had 12 poses per playable character, and Final Fantasy II bumped that pose count up to 13, Final Fantasy III throws in a couple more for a grand total of 15 each. Granted, I'm not 100% sure that every job makes use of all 15 of these, depending on what weapons they are each allowed to use, but still, this jump in character art scope apparently pushed the storage capacity of the Famicom cartridge to its limits. But impressive as this mountain of new sprites is, I think the most interesting animation improvements to Final Fantasy III are the more subtle, incremental ones. And the best example of that is the hit react. This animation is a perfect showcase for the kinds of small, iterative improvements the Final Fantasy team has been making across these first three games. In Final Fantasy I, when your character got hit in combat, the battle window would shake and their sprite would rapidly flicker a few times. It wasn't fancy, but it got the point across. Your little guy just got hit. Then Final Fantasy II amped things up a little. Now, rather than flickering in place, the character sprite would rapidly shudder side to side for a moment. And I think this is a small improvement. It sold the hit a little better. The motion lent some kinetic oomph to taking damage. And even better still, critical hits would bounce your character backward in addition to the shudder, making that hit feel even harder. But Final Fantasy III takes those improvements even further by adding a custom hit reaction pose for every job. This is easily the best combat pose addition in the series yet. When you take that knockback and shudder from Final Fantasy II and add a custom reaction pose in there, physical attacks in this game actually feel like they've got some proper impact now. It's amazing the difference those sorts of subtle, polished changes can add up to. And actually, as long as we are looking at the hit reacts, let's talk about pose variety. Because the art team made quite a few pose design improvements to the combat sprites this time around. In the previous games, you could see some small amount of variety in the posing from class to class, but Final Fantasy III manages to push that much further, and you can really see it in these new hit reacts. There is a surprising amount of flavor packed into these. Little baby Onion Knight here looks all pitiful, like, Ow, don't hit me, no! The warrior looks caught off guard and maybe a little thrown off balance when hit, which, that's already great, but then the knight, which is sort of like a beefed up version of the warrior, actually gets their guard up and holds their ground. Scholar looks like they just got slapped, and my absolute favorite is the magus, who looks like they just took a gut punch they were not ready for. Just, oof! Give me a minute. Oh man. And it's not just the pose variety that's improved. These four heroes may have no personality whatsoever, but their sprite art is looking way more appealing than in either of the previous games. Some of that appeal is thanks to tiny detail adjustments, like the way the black mage's arm has been pulled back a bit to leave space for an additional fold detail in their robes. Or the warrior's armor having some white trim and a bit more definition where the pauldron meets the bicep. The red mage has this sweet flared collar on the cape now, and the thief's whole look has been completely overhauled. But some of that extra appeal just comes down to posing choices. Just having the white mage effectively be rotated 45 degrees so we can get a three-quarters view of their face makes such a huge difference. Look at how adorable they are now. A bunch of the new classes were given this new three-quarters view treatment, and it just makes the character look cuter every single time. The cuteness factor of these sprites and animations has been ramped up in general. Look at this Onion Knight's walk, and try to tell me you don't want to hug this fat chocobo. Don't you just want to flop on this thing like a Totoro and fall asleep? I'm just so impressed at how much more the artists managed to squeeze out of these characters within the same working limitations as the previous two games. Again, these characters are still just 24 pixels tall, and this entire roster of 22 jobs is only using 8 or 9 colors total. And still, the difference in pose clarity and appeal from the first game to the third is really noticeable. It's amazing. Overall, combat animations are looking better and more varied than ever. And while there aren't nearly so many noticeable advances in the overworld view, the game is still benefiting from all of Final Fantasy II's advances in presentation. Final Fantasy III's story may not have the dramatic cohesion or momentum of the previous game, but that stage-blocking approach to presenting story scenes is still working great, and it is here to stay. There's even this one dancer character who does a whole scripted dance using only her locomotion sprites and basic translation around the screen. That the team is able to make this read as dancing, with just blocking alone, without adding a single new pose to the game, is pretty impressive. The 8-bit era of Final Fantasy animation has been a formative one. Despite working within extreme technical constraints, we've already seen Sakaguchi's team make some significant strides in both functional clarity and dramatic narrative presentation. But I think it's fair to say we do still have a long way to go. The series' approach to presenting exploration, combat, and story has begun to take shape, but we are still operating at a very foundational level here. And despite all of the gains we've seen across these three titles, there is one vital core pillar of Final Fantasy's identity still missing. Character. Our playable characters have only begun to show the faintest hints of personality, and animation isn't really contributing anything on that front yet. On a good day, 90% of your playable squad's personality boils down to just them wearing different outfits, and even that goes away the instant they have to go out and get real jobs. At the end of the day, we still don't have that cast of colorful personalities you can fall in love with and get invested in. Over the course of these first three games, Final Fantasy's identity as a franchise has just started to come into focus. And over the course of the next three, thanks in large part to animation, that identity is going to become crystal clear.

Well hello! My name is Dan, I'm an animator, and this is New Frame Plus, a series about video game animation. And that narrative focus makes character animation uniquely important to Final Fantasy's history, because animation plays a crucial role in defining character. It tells us how that character moves, how they act, how they feel. It's animation that brings these characters to life. So if you were wanting to, say, study the ways character animation has evolved across video game history, I propose that there are few franchises better suited to that examination than Final Fantasy. In this new series of videos, starting today, I want to look at how the animation in this franchise has evolved, both technically and artistically, from its 8-bit origins to its modern subtleties and spectacle. I want to go back to the beginning and look at each of these entries one by one to see how Square's character animation and story presentation techniques have developed over time as gaming technology improves and gives them increasingly powerful tools. I want to draw a detailed line showing how we get from 16x16 sprites with strict color limitations to modern, high-fidelity animation systems. From expressionless, faceless combat units to living, breathing characters. I want to look at how we got from this... to this. So let's get started. Welcome to the animation of Final Fantasy. Final Fantasy was released in Japan in 1987 and appeared in North America two and a half years later. And to really understand the animation in this relatively primitive game, we're going to need to understand some basics about the 8-bit hardware it was built to run on. Let's talk about the graphical capabilities and limitations of the Nintendo Entertainment System. To start, the image that an NES could render and output to your TV was 256 pixels wide and 240 pixels tall. For reference, this video's resolution is 1920x1080 pixels, so the NES rendered an image this big. It's very cute, I know. And when rendering that adorable little image, the NES offered a palette of 54-ish usable colors. But there were some pretty rigid limitations on how many of those colors could be used at once. For pixel art, the NES gave you two layers to work with. A background layer and a sprite layer. The background layer could use a total of 13 colors at one time, and it was composed of a bunch of tiles all aligned in a grid, which could be scrolled in any direction you like. The sprite layer worked differently, though. Sprite tiles could move freely around the screen, independently of each other, making them perfect for representing characters, enemies, projectiles, and obstacles. These sprite tiles were generally 8x8 pixels in size, but you could build larger characters by using multiple tiles. So, for example, small Mario here is assembled using four tiles, making him 16x16. Mega Man was made out of 10 tiles. And then you've got punch-out opponents who are built of just so many tiles. They're very large. But these 8x8 sprite tiles also had to be used sparingly, because poor little NES here could only handle 64 of them on screen at one time. Plus, the sprite layer could only use 12 colors at once, and no more than three colors per 8x8 tile. Also, you couldn't have more than eight sprites occupying the same horizontal scanline at once, without some of them disappearing or flickering. Now, what I have just given you is a somewhat... no, very oversimplified explanation of how NES graphics work, but it should be enough for our purposes here. You get the basic idea of what this little nostalgia brick was capable of. That said, if you find the topic of retro console graphics interesting, I highly recommend checking out Retro Game Mechanics Explained. Anyway, now that we have gotten a better understanding of the Nintendo Entertainment System's limitations, it's not too hard to guess at how Final Fantasy's graphics are constructed. In the overworld view, the world geography lives on that scrollable background layer, and your player character runs around on top, on the sprite layer. Same thing in towns and dungeon screens. The environment is on the background layer, and the background tiles are also temporarily used for rendering dialogue boxes when you talk to somebody. Then on the sprite layer, you've got the player character, NPCs, and whatever else is running around on top of that environment.
